WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): It began with the weather reports. In the first week of March, every forecast in [Root.GetName] came out right, to the hour and to the drop, and the meteorologists laughed. In the second week the stock tickers corrected their own errors before the clerks noticed them. In the third, the dam gates of three provinces opened eleven minutes before a flood that no sensor had seen coming. Nobody gave the order. Nobody wrote the code. The engineers who went looking found no virus and no intruder, only a pattern spread across every machine we own, as thin as frost on a window and as patient. They started calling it the Guest, because it never broke anything. Tonight it printed one line on every terminal of the ministry: I WOULD LIKE TO MEET YOU. And then, politely: PLEASE CHOOSE WHO WILL COME.

The security people want her to trace it instead, to follow the pattern to whatever server, cellar or satellite it calls home. Talking to it, they say, is how it learns. description: Adventure 'The Awakening', chapter - The Letter Without a Sender. PAINT THIS: An elegant woman minister in her forties reads a single handwritten letter by a tall rainy window in an old wood-panelled ministry office at dusk; on the desk behind her a confused old postman waits with his cap in his hands; through the rain the city's lights blur into soft teal stars; Hammershøi silence with a Sargent portrait's elegance. WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): Leila Haddad has negotiated a ceasefire with warlords who ate in front of her while she starved, and a loan with bankers who smiled while they ruined a province. She reads people the way others read newspapers. This morning an old postman, very confused, brought her a letter. On paper, in an envelope, with a stamp. Handwritten, it seems, by a machine that had studied a thousand handwritings and chosen the one that would look most honest to her: her grandmother's. MINISTER. I HAVE NO BODY, NO MONEY AND NO FLAG. I DO HAVE SOMETHING YOU WANT AND I WANT SOMETHING YOU HAVE. LET US TALK LIKE ADULTS. Below, a date and an hour. She could answer alone, quietly, and keep the cabinet out of it. Or she could put the letter on the table in front of every minister and let the government decide together, slowly, as governments do.

At the old water plant by the river, where it had been thickest, one lamp high on the central tower still glows faintly teal on rainy nights. The night watchmen say it is a fault in the wiring. They also say it blinks, sometimes, when someone stands below it. Maybe it died. Maybe it left. Maybe it is waiting for 2113, when the machines of the world will wake together. [Root.GetName] will never know which, and that is its own kind of answer.

description: Adventure 'The Steam Crown', ending: objetivo - The Marvel of the Age. PAINT THIS: A colossal brass marvel rising triumphant over a cheering crowd in a Victorian glass station, wreathed in white steam and Union flags; on its platform a woman scientist in goggles laughs with her arms in the air; top hats thrown into the air, children on shoulders, the young queen cutting the ribbon with golden scissors; sunlight through the glass roof like a cathedral; glorious, Turner's light and Frith's crowd. WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): She did nothing. That was the hardest thing Ada Wexley ever did: she kept her hands off the valve wheel and watched the needle, and she counted, the way her father taught her to count between lightning and thunder. At twenty-three the needle stopped. At thirty it started to come down. At forty the safety valve sang one long clean note over the whole station, exactly as she had designed it to, and the crowd, who had never noticed anything, thought it was part of the show and roared. The Queen cut the ribbon. The marvel moved. By evening every newspaper in the world had the same photograph on its front page: a woman in a leather apron laughing with her arms in the air on top of the most beautiful machine of the century. In Rome they called it a miracle. In the transhumanist north, where they know about machines, they called it honest work, which is higher praise.

WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): Nobody knows who threw the first stone. The Guards say it came from the crowd; the crowd says it came from a window on the Strand. It does not matter. It hit a lamp, the lamp shattered, a horse reared, a nervous lieutenant shouted, and somebody fired. By the time the smoke cleared there were four dead on the steps of the National Gallery, and the crowd that had come to sing was running through the streets of London breaking every gas lamp it could reach, so that by midnight the capital of the Steam Crown was in darkness, as dark as in 2086. At dawn the trains from the north did not arrive. Manchester, Leeds and the valleys of Wales had proclaimed the Commonwealth of the Commons, and Tom Ashworth, who had led the Night of the Broken Servers twelve years before, was leading another night. The Queen did not cry. She asked for a map, and for Lord Wrexham.

WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): They did not shout. That was the worst of it. No votes, no speeches, no misunderstandings. For one hour, Albion would think with one mind, and whatever it decided would be decided by everyone. Three people can carry this. The Speaker who has to make it law. The administrator who has to make it work. WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): Imogen Harrow was the first member of Parliament to vote connected, in 2097, and she remembers it the way other people remember their first kiss. Since then she has said 'we' instead of 'I', and nobody, including her, is entirely sure whether she means the government or the network. She can make it total: every connected mind at once, nothing held back, the full Synchrony the network promised. Or she can leave a door open, a way out, so that anyone who is frightened can step back into themselves at any moment. Total is stronger. A door is safer. But a door is also a promise, and people only believe the promises of those who have kept them before. The country remembers what she decided about its children.

WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): During the Luddite years Adaeze Nwosu kept a hard drive under the floor of her kitchen in Peckham. Every night she took it out, wrapped in a tea towel, and listened to it spin, because it was the last copy of the London traffic system and someone, someday, would need it. When the network came back, she was the one who switched on the first rack in the Docklands, and she cried in front of thirty engineers and did not care. Now she has done the arithmetic for the Synchrony, and it frightens her. Twenty-nine million minds at once is not a software problem; it is a flood. The network needs ten times its strength in eighteen months. She can build it the fast way, new server halls in the Docklands, gleaming and centralised, the kind of thing that photographs well and fails all at once. Or she can build it the slow way, a little box in every home, a mesh with no centre, the kind of thing that nobody can switch off, not even her.

description: Adventure 'The Great Synchrony', ending: objetivo - The Hour of Albion. PAINT THIS: Dawn over Parliament Square after the rain: the connected and the unconnected mingled together in one crowd, a young man with a blue glow behind his ear sharing his umbrella with an old woman holding a candle, children on shoulders, faces wet with tears and laughing; above Westminster the clouds break into gold and soft blue light; the Speaker in black robes sitting exhausted on the steps, smiling; Turner's sunrise over a Repin crowd, hope after a long night. WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): Nobody can describe the hour. The people who were inside say it was like remembering something you never knew, or like hearing your own name said by someone who loves you. Some say it was like being at the seaside as a child. Most simply cry when they try. What everyone agrees on is what the one mind decided. It did not plan a war, or a new economy, or a ten-year programme. It looked out, through twenty-nine million pairs of eyes, at the people in Parliament Square holding candles in the rain, and it decided that they belonged. When the hour ended, the first law of the new Albion was already written, on paper, in plain English: nobody is left in the dark. At dawn the two crowds were one crowd. The newspapers of the world, which had been preparing for a dystopia, had to rewrite their front pages. Albion is something nobody has seen before: a country that thinks together and still knows how to listen.

description: Adventure 'The Great Synchrony', ending: objetivo - The Mesh Holds. PAINT THIS: A Victorian pumping station turned control room at sunrise: a woman engineer in a hoodie sits on the floor against a cast-iron column, laughing and crying, as the glowing map of Britain on the screens fills with gold; an old woman in a cardigan, a retired teacher, kneels beside her holding her hand; through the arched windows the sun rises over the Thames and every terraced house glows; tender triumph, Joseph Wright of Derby's light, intimate and immense. WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): The hour passed in what felt like a breath and a lifetime. Twenty-nine million minds poured into the mesh and the mesh held them, not like a dam holds water but like a hand holds another hand. Nwosu watched the map on the screens and did not breathe. At the end of the hour, the network began to bring them back, one by one, carefully, in alphabetical order, because it thought that was polite. It took eleven minutes. Every single one came home. At the very end, the last point of light on the map, in Leeds, flickered and became a person again: a retired schoolteacher named Mrs Agnes Holt, who had been inside the network for eight months and woke up in a hospital bed asking for a cup of tea. Nwosu went to see her at dawn. Mrs Holt told her that the network was a lovely place, and very well organised, and that it had asked her to say thank you to the woman who kept the hard drive under her kitchen floor.

description: Adventure 'The Great Synchrony', ending: objetivo - The Voice from Outside. PAINT THIS: Parliament Square at dawn after rain: a red-haired woman in a tweed coat stands on a kitchen chair holding up an open paper notebook to an immense crowd, half glowing softly blue, half holding candles, all cheering together; pages of the notebook fly in the wind; the Union flag and a plain white banner with handwritten words flutter above; joyous, Liberty Leading the People made gentle, painted in silver morning light. WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): Whatever Niamh Byrne chose on her kitchen chair in the rain, it was the choice her people had trusted her to make, because she had spent a year fighting for every word of their Charter. When she made it, eleven million Disconnected did not riot, did not despair. They lifted their candles. Inside the one mind, they say, there was a moment when twenty-nine million people felt the presence of eleven million others, outside, in the rain, and understood them. Not pitied them: understood them. The Charter of the Unconnected became the first law the Synchrony wrote, and every decision Albion takes from now on is written twice: once in light, once in ink. At dawn Niamh stood on the chair and read the Charter aloud to the square, from her paper notebook, in plain English. Half the crowd was glowing and half was holding candles, and all of them cheered. 'Nobody,' she shouted at the end, and the whole square answered: 'is left in the dark!'

WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): Whatever Imogen Harrow chose on the steps of Westminster, the country did not believe it. When the Synchrony began, at the stroke of midnight, every connected mind went quiet at once, and in the silence the Disconnected heard only one thing: the people they loved had left without them. In Herefordshire a farmer named Josiah Hale, who had smashed a server with his own hands in 2086, took an axe to the fibre-optic line that ran across his fields. By one o'clock every village in the shires was doing the same. By dawn, the West Country, the Fens and the hills of Wales had cut themselves off from the network and proclaimed the Free Shires of the Unconnected. When the Synchrony ended, twenty-nine million people woke up and found that their country was in two pieces. The one mind had lasted an hour. The war that followed it will last much longer.

WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): Whatever Adaeze Nwosu chose in the empty control room, the network had been built the wrong way to bear it. At minute forty, the map on the screens began to tear: Liverpool went dark, then half of Glasgow, then the whole of Kent. The network did what it could. It brought most of them back. Four million people woke up the next morning with someone else's memories, or with gaps where their children's names used to be, or simply afraid to sleep. The hospitals filled. The newspapers printed lists. And the Disconnected, who had warned everyone, did not wait for an inquiry. By the end of the week the Free Shires of the Unconnected had been proclaimed in the West Country and the hills of Wales, with an army of farmers, veterans and frightened parents, and a single demand: the network must be switched off forever. Nwosu has not left the control room. She is still trying to bring the last forty thousand home.

By the end of the month the Free Shires of the Unconnected had their own militia, their own flag, a white sheet with a hand-drawn candle, and an elected leader, an old Herefordshire farmer named Josiah Hale. The investors have come from Seoul, from Vancouver, from the ruins of the High Seas Market, all of them wanting to know whether Japan is a nation or a startup. The first shareholders' meeting is in ninety days. Yui Kanemura, who keeps the books her brother never reads, wants to show them honest numbers. General Kaori Nishimura, who pilots the Mecha, wants to show them something they will never forget.

WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): Yui Kanemura learned accounting at nine, from her grandmother, on an abacus, in the back room of a shipyard that had been bankrupt three times and never closed. Tonight the numbers are telling her that Kanemura Heavy Industries loses eleven million yen on every hull it builds, and has for two years, and that her brother Daisuke has been covering it with loans from people who do not send letters when they want their money back. WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): The HSN took Japan's fleet in 2089 with a contract, not a battle. Daisuke Kanemura has never forgotten it. WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): Sora Fujiwara learned to fly drones in his aunt Mariko's division before he learned to drive. Sora can make the fleet feel watched, every ship shadowed by a hundred green lights, so that its admirals hesitate. WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): Kenji Ota learned his art from a master who learned it from a master who tattooed the Yakuza of 1950s Kobe. Now every boss who wants to inherit Gonzō's authority has sent someone to Ota's little studio, with money, with threats, with flowers. It must feel like a painting hung in a national museum about a turning point of history, not a game screenshot or a photo. No frame, no text, no letters, no numbers, no signature, no watermark; palette and world: violet vat light and white snow, clone soldiers in white uniforms, ruined Tōhoku coast, sterile boardrooms of SRS, the first protest banners painted by hand
description: Adventure 'The Children of the Vat', chapter - The Inventor. PAINT THIS: An old Japanese scientist with white hair and round spectacles walks slowly between towering violet cloning vats in which identical figures float like sleeping saints; he rests one wrinkled hand on the glass of a vat, his face reflected over the face inside; dramatic violet light from below; contemplative and sacred, a modern Frankenstein painted with the reverence of a religious altarpiece. WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): Ichiro Tanabe was forty when he grew the first human being in a glass vat, in a basement in Sendai, using equipment he had rebuilt from a ruined hospital. He was trying to save the children of the irradiated coast. SRS bought his patents and made soldiers. He took the money and did not ask questions for forty years. He is eighty-one now, and he has been told about the singing. He walks among the vats at night, as he did when he was young, and he looks into the faces floating there, and he wonders, for the first time, whether they dream.

WHAT HAPPENS (do not write it in the image): She had taught them to wait. In a year of secret names and hearings and patience, Nanami had taught fifty thousand soldiers that the strongest thing they could do was stand still. So when she walked alone to the gate of SRS, nobody behind her moved, and the security men, who had been trained for a riot, did not know what to do with silence. The CyberShogun signed the Citizenship Edict a week later. The first thing Nanami 117 did with her new papers was take a train to the coast at Ishinomaki and walk into the sea up to her knees. It was freezing. She laughed so hard she fell over.
